because of the high computational cost of the projection. We remark that S3CCM needs much fewer iterations. However, the iteration cost is much better for our algorithm which proves to be an advantage on large datasets. On smaller datasets (such as MovieLens100k), projection based methods such as S3CCM would be faster.

14
Proofs

We first prove some key lemmas. This section builds on top of the analysis of
[
MHK20
]
and the homotopy CGM framework of
[
Yur+18
]
.
All these results are for the inexact oracle with additive error, the exact oracle case can be obtained setting
\delta=0
.

First, we adapt the definition of the additive error
lmo – additive
to the smoothed objective
F_{\beta_{k}}
.
At iteration
k
, for the given
v_{k}
, we assume that the approximate
lmo
returns an element
\tilde{s}_{k}\in\mathcal{X}
such that:

\big\langle v_{k},\,\tilde{s}_{k}\big\rangle\leq\big\langle v_{k},\,s_{k}\big\rangle+\delta\frac{\eta_{k}}{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\left(L_{f}+\frac{\|A\|^{2}}{\beta_{k}}\right)

(19)

for some
\delta>0
, where
s_{k}
is the exact
lmo
solution.

Lemma 3
.

For any given iteration
k\geq 1
of Algorithm
7
the following relation holds:

\displaystyle\big\langle\nabla F_{\beta_{k}}(\theta_{k}),\,s_{k}-\theta_{k}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla_{\theta}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k},\omega)-d_{k}\|D_{\mathcal{X}}+f^{\star}-{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k},\omega)+g(A\theta^{\star})-g_{\beta_{k}}(A\theta_{k})

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{\beta_{k}}{2}\|y^{\ast}_{\beta_{k}}(A\theta_{k})\|^{2}+\delta\frac{\eta_{k}}{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\left(L_{f}+\frac{\|A\|^{2}}{\beta_{k}}\right)

where
\delta\geq 0
is the accuracy of the inexact
lmo
with additive error as in Equation (
19
).

Proof.

\displaystyle\big\langle\nabla F_{\beta_{k}}

\displaystyle(\theta_{k}),\,\tilde{s}_{k}-\theta_{k}\big\rangle=\big\langle\nabla_{\theta}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k},\omega),\,\tilde{s}_{k}-\theta_{k}\big\rangle+\big\langle A^{\top}\nabla g_{\beta_{k}}(A\theta_{k}),\,\tilde{s}_{k}-\theta_{k}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle\nabla_{\theta}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k},\omega)-d_{k},\,\tilde{s}_{k}-\theta_{k}\big\rangle+\big\langle d_{k}+A^{\top}\nabla g_{\beta_{k}}(A\theta_{k}),\,\tilde{s}_{k}-\theta_{k}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\big\langle\nabla_{\theta}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k},\omega)-d_{k},\,s_{k}-\theta_{k}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad+\big\langle d_{k}+A^{\top}\nabla g_{\beta_{k}}(A\theta_{k}),\,s_{k}-\theta_{k}\big\rangle+\delta\frac{\eta_{k}}{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\left(L_{f}+\frac{\|A\|^{2}}{\beta_{k}}\right)

(20)

\displaystyle\leq\big\langle\nabla_{\theta}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k},\omega)-d_{k},\,s_{k}-\theta_{k}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad+\big\langle d_{k}+A^{\top}\nabla g_{\beta_{k}}(A\theta_{k}),\,\theta^{\star}-\theta_{k}\big\rangle+\delta\frac{\eta_{k}}{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\left(L_{f}+\frac{\|A\|^{2}}{\beta_{k}}\right)

(21)

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla_{\theta}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k},\omega)-d_{k}\|\|s_{k}-\theta^{\star}\|

\displaystyle\qquad+\big\langle\nabla_{\theta}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k},\omega)+A^{\top}\nabla g_{\beta_{k}}(A\theta_{k}),\,\theta^{\star}-\theta_{k}\big\rangle+\delta\frac{\eta_{k}}{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\left(L_{f}+\frac{\|A\|^{2}}{\beta_{k}}\right)

(22)

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla_{\theta}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k},\omega)-d_{k}\|D_{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\big\langle\nabla_{\theta}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k},\omega)+A^{\top}\nabla g_{\beta_{k}}(A\theta_{k}),\,\theta^{\star}-\theta_{k}\big\rangle+\delta\frac{\eta_{k}}{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\left(L_{f}+\frac{\|A\|^{2}}{\beta_{k}}\right)

(23)

where Equation (
20
) is the definition of inexact oracle with additive error, Equation (
21
) follows from
s_{k}
being a solution of
\min_{\theta\in\mathcal{X}}\big\langle d_{k}+A^{\top}\nabla g_{\beta_{k}}(A\theta_{k}),\,\theta\big\rangle
, Equation (
22
) is Cauchy-Schwarz and the Equation (
23
) is the definition of diameter.
Now, convexity of
{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k},\omega)
ensures
\langle\nabla_{\theta}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k},\omega),\theta^{\star}-\theta_{k}\rangle\leq f^{\star}-{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k},\omega)
.

From Lemma 10 in
[
TDFC18
]
we have that:

\displaystyle g(z_{1})

\displaystyle\geq g_{\beta}(z_{2})+\big\langle\nabla g_{\beta}(z_{2}),\,z_{1}-z_{2}\big\rangle+\frac{\beta}{2}\|y^{\ast}_{\beta}(z_{2})\|^{2}.

(24)

Therefore:

\displaystyle\big\langle A^{\top}\nabla g_{\beta_{k}}(A\theta_{k}),\,\theta^{\star}-\theta_{k}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\leq g(A\theta^{\star})-g_{\beta_{k}}(A\theta_{k})-\frac{\beta_{k}}{2}\|y^{\ast}_{\beta_{k}}(A\theta_{k})\|^{2}.

Which yields the desired result:

\displaystyle\big\langle\nabla F_{\beta_{k}}(\theta_{k}),\,\tilde{s}_{k}-\theta_{k}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla_{\theta}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k},\omega)-d_{k}\|D_{\mathcal{X}}+f^{\star})-{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k},\omega)+g(A\theta^{\star})-g_{\beta_{k}}(A\theta_{k})

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{\beta_{k}}{2}\|y^{\ast}_{\beta_{k}}(A\theta_{k})\|^{2}+\delta\frac{\eta_{k}}{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\left(L_{f}+\frac{\|A\|^{2}}{\beta_{k}}\right)

∎

Lemma 4
.

For any
k\geq 1
the estimate of the gradient computed in Algorithm
7
satisfies:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|\nabla_{\theta}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k},\omega)-d_{k}\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{Q}{(k+8)^{\frac{2}{3}}}

where
Q=\max\left\{\|\nabla_{\theta}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{1},\omega)-d_{1}\|^{2}7^{\frac{2}{3}},16\sigma^{2}+81L_{f}^{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\right\}

Proof.

This lemma applies Lemma 1 and Lemma 17 of
[
MHK20
]
to our different stepsize sequences. First, we invoke Lemma 1:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\left[\|\nabla_{\theta}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k},\omega)-d_{k}\|^{2}\right]\leq\left(1-\frac{\rho_{k}}{2}\right)\|\nabla_{\theta}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k-1},\omega)-d_{k-1}\|^{2}+\rho_{k}^{2}\sigma^{2}+\frac{2L_{f}^{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\eta_{k-1}^{2}}{\rho_{k}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{2}{(k+7)^{\frac{2}{3}}}\right)\|\nabla_{\theta}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k-1},\omega)-d_{k-1}\|^{2}+\frac{16\sigma^{2}+81L_{f}^{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}}{(k+7)^{\frac{4}{3}}}

(25)

where we used
\rho_{k}=\frac{4}{(k+7)^{\frac{2}{3}}}
.
Applying Lemma 17 of
[
MHK20
]
to Equation (
25
) with
k_{0}=7
,
\alpha=\frac{2}{3}
,
c=2
,
b=16\sigma^{2}+81L_{f}^{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}
gives:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|\nabla_{\theta}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k},\omega)-d_{k}\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{Q}{(k+8)^{\frac{2}{3}}}

where
Q=\max\left\{\|\nabla{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(x_{1},\omega)-d_{1}\|^{2}7^{\frac{2}{3}},16\sigma^{2}+81L_{f}^{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\right\}

∎

14.1
Proof of Theorem
5

We prove Theorem
5
with the oracle with additive error. The proof without additive error can be obtained with
\delta=0
.

Theorem 5
.

The sequence
\theta_{k}
generated by Algorithm
7
satisfies the following bound for
k\geq 1
:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}F_{\beta_{k}}(\theta_{k+1})-F^{\star}\leq 9^{\frac{1}{3}}\frac{C_{\delta}}{(k+8)^{\frac{1}{3}}},

where
C_{\delta}:=\frac{81}{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}(L_{f}+\beta_{0}\|A\|^{2})(1+\delta)+9D_{\mathcal{X}}\sqrt{Q}
,

Q=\max\left\{4\|\nabla{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{1},\omega)-d_{1}\|^{2},16\sigma^{2}+2L_{f}^{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\right\}
and
\delta\geq 0
.

Proof.

First, we use the smoothness of
F_{\beta_{k}}
to upper bound the progress.
Note that
F_{\beta_{k}}
is
(L_{f}+\|A\|^{2}/\beta_{k})
-smooth.

\displaystyle F_{\beta_{k}}(\theta_{k+1})

\displaystyle\leq F_{\beta_{k}}(\theta_{k})+\eta_{k}\big\langle\nabla F_{\beta_{k}}(\theta_{k}),\,\tilde{s}_{k}-\theta_{k}\big\rangle+\frac{\eta_{k}^{2}}{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}(L_{f}+\frac{\|A\|^{2}}{\beta_{k}}),

where
s_{k}\in\mathcal{X}
denotes the atom selected by the
lmo
.
We now apply Lemma
3
and obtain:

\displaystyle F_{\beta_{k}}(\theta_{k+1})

\displaystyle\leq F_{\beta_{k}}(\theta_{k})+\eta_{k}\left(f^{\star}-{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k},\omega)+g(A\theta^{\star})-g_{\beta_{k}}(A\theta_{k})-\frac{\beta_{k}}{2}\|\nabla y^{\ast}_{\beta_{k}}(A\theta_{k})\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\eta_{k}^{2}}{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}(L_{f}+\frac{\|A\|^{2}}{\beta_{k}})(1+\delta)+\eta_{k}\|\nabla_{\theta}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k},\omega)-d_{k}\|D_{\mathcal{X}}.

(26)

Now, using Lemma 10 of
[
TDFC18
]
we get:

g_{\beta}(z_{1})\leq g_{\gamma}(z_{1})+\frac{\gamma-\beta}{2}\|y^{\ast}_{\beta}(z_{1})\|^{2}

(27)

and therefore:

\displaystyle F_{\beta_{k}}(\theta_{k})

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k},\omega)+g_{\beta_{k}}(A\theta_{k})

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k},\omega)+g_{\beta_{k-1}}(A\theta_{k})+\frac{\beta_{k-1}-\beta_{k}}{2}\|y^{\ast}_{\beta_{k}}(A\theta_{k})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=F_{\beta_{k-1}}(\theta_{k})+\frac{\beta_{k-1}-\beta_{k}}{2}\|y^{\ast}_{\beta_{k}}(A\theta_{k})\|^{2}.

We combine this with (
26
) and subtract
F^{\star}
from both sides to get

\displaystyle F_{\beta_{k}}(\theta_{k+1})-F^{\star}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\eta_{k})\big(F_{\beta_{k-1}}(\theta_{k})-F^{\star}\big)+\frac{\eta_{k}^{2}}{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}(L_{f}+\frac{\|A\|^{2}}{\beta_{k}})(1+\delta)

\displaystyle+\big((1-\eta_{k})(\beta_{k-1}-\beta_{k})-\eta_{k}\beta_{k}\big)\frac{1}{2}\|y^{\ast}_{\beta_{k}}(A\theta_{k})\|^{2}+\eta_{k}\|\nabla_{\theta}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k},\omega)-d_{k}\|D_{\mathcal{X}}.

Let us choose
\eta_{k}
and
\beta_{k}
in a way to vanish the last term.
By choosing
\eta_{k}=\frac{9}{k+8}
and
\beta_{k}=\frac{\beta_{0}}{(k+8)^{\frac{1}{2}}}
for
k\geq 1
with some
\beta_{0}>0
, we get
(1-\eta_{k})(\beta_{k-1}-\beta_{k})-\eta_{k}\beta_{k}<0
. Hence, we end up with

\displaystyle F_{\beta_{k}}(\theta_{k+1})-F^{\star}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\eta_{k})\big(F_{\beta_{k-1}}(\theta_{k})-F^{\star}\big)+\frac{\eta_{k}^{2}}{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}(L_{f}+\frac{\|A\|^{2}}{\beta_{k}})(1+\delta)

\displaystyle\qquad+\eta_{k}\|\nabla_{\theta}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k},\omega)-d_{k}\|D_{\mathcal{X}}.

We now compute the expectation and use Jensen inequality and Lemma
4
to obtain the final recursion:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}F_{\beta_{k}}(\theta_{k+1})-F^{\star}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\eta_{k})\big(\mathbb{E}F_{\beta_{k-1}}(\theta_{k})-F^{\star}\big)+\frac{\eta_{k}^{2}}{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}(L_{f}+\frac{\|A\|^{2}}{\beta_{k}})(1+\delta)+\frac{9D_{\mathcal{X}}\sqrt{Q}}{(k+8)^{\frac{4}{3}}}

Now, note that:

\displaystyle\frac{\eta_{k}^{2}}{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}(L_{f}+\frac{\|A\|^{2}}{\beta_{k}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{k}^{2}}{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}L_{f}+\frac{\eta_{k}^{2}}{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\frac{\|A\|^{2}}{\beta_{k}}\leq\frac{\frac{81}{2}}{(k+8)^{\frac{4}{3}}}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}L_{f}+\frac{\frac{81}{2}}{(k+8)^{\frac{4}{3}}}\beta_{0}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\|A\|^{2}

Therefore:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}F_{\beta_{k}}(\theta_{k+1})-F^{\star}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{9}{k+8}\right)\big(\mathbb{E}F_{\beta_{k-1}}(\theta_{k})-F^{\star}\big)

\displaystyle+\frac{\frac{81}{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}(L_{f}+\beta_{0}\|A\|^{2})(1+\delta)+9D_{\mathcal{X}}\sqrt{Q}}{(k+8)^{\frac{4}{3}}}

For simplicity, let
C_{\delta}:=\frac{81}{2}D_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}(L_{f}+\beta_{0}\|A\|^{2})(1+\delta)+9D_{\mathcal{X}}\sqrt{Q}
and
\mathcal{E}_{k+1}:=\mathbb{E}F_{\beta_{k}}(\theta_{k+1})-F^{\star}
. Then, we need to solve the following recursive equation:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{k+1}\leq\left(1-\frac{9}{k+8}\right)\mathcal{E}_{k}+\frac{C_{\delta}}{(k+8)^{\frac{4}{3}}}

(28)

Let the induction hypothesis for
k\geq 1
be:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{k+1}\leq 9^{\frac{1}{3}}\frac{C_{\delta}}{(k+8)^{\frac{1}{3}}}

For the base case
k=1
we need to prove
\mathcal{E}_{2}\leq C_{\delta}
.
From Equation (
28
) we have
\mathcal{E}_{2}\leq\frac{C_{\delta}}{(9)^{\frac{4}{3}}}<C_{\delta}
as
9^{\frac{4}{3}}>1

Now:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{k+1}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{9}{k+8}\right)\mathcal{E}_{k}+\frac{C_{\delta}}{(k+8)^{\frac{4}{3}}}\leq\left(1-\frac{9}{k+8}\right)9^{\frac{1}{3}}\frac{C_{\delta}}{(k+7)^{\frac{1}{3}}}+\frac{C_{\delta}}{(k+8)^{\frac{4}{3}}}\leq 9^{\frac{1}{3}}\frac{C_{\delta}}{(k+8)^{\frac{1}{3}}}

∎

14.2
Proof of Theorem
1

After proving Theorem
5
, the proof of Theorem
1
follows easily using arguments from
[
Yur+18
]
and setting
\delta=0
.

If
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
is
L_{g}
-Lipschitz continuous from equation (2.7) in
[
Nes05
]
and the duality between Lipshitzness and bounded support (
cf.
Lemma 5 in
[
D“”+16
]
) we have:

g_{\beta}(z)\leq g(z)\leq g_{\beta}(z)+\frac{\beta}{2}L_{g}^{2}

(29)

Using this fact, we write:

\displaystyle g(A\theta_{k+1})

\displaystyle\leq g_{\beta_{k}}(A\theta_{k+1})+\frac{\beta_{k}L_{g}^{2}}{2}=g_{\beta_{k}}(A\theta_{k+1})+\frac{\beta_{0}L_{g}^{2}}{2\sqrt{k+8}}.

We complete the proof by adding
{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k+1},\omega)-F^{\star}
to both sides:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}F(\theta_{k+1})-F^{\star}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}F_{\beta_{k}}(\theta_{k+1})-F^{\star}+\frac{\beta_{0}L_{g}^{2}}{2\sqrt{k+8}}\leq 9^{\frac{1}{3}}\frac{C_{\delta}}{(k+8)^{\frac{1}{3}}}+\frac{\beta_{0}L_{g}^{2}}{2\sqrt{k+8}}.

14.3
Proof of Theorem
2

We adapt to our setting the proof technique of Theorem 4.3 in
[
Yur+18
]
. We give the proof for the more general case of additive errors and obtain the proof for Theorem
2
as a special case with
\delta=0
.

From the Lagrange saddle point theory, we know that the following bound holds
\forall\theta\in\mathcal{X}
and
\forall r\in\mathcal{K}
:

\displaystyle f^{\star}\leq\mathcal{L}(\theta,r,y^{\star})

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta,\omega)+\big\langle y_{\star},\,A\theta-r\big\rangle\leq{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta,\omega)+\|y_{\star}\|\|A\theta-r\|,

Since
\theta_{k+1}\in\mathcal{X}
and taking the expectation, we get

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k+1},\omega)-f^{\star}

\displaystyle\geq-{\mathbb{E}}\min_{r\in\mathcal{K}}\|y^{\star}\|\|A\theta_{k+1}-r\|=-\|y^{\star}\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathrm{dist}(A\theta_{k+1},\mathcal{K}).

(30)

This proves the first bound in Theorem
2
.

The second bound directly follows by Theorem
5

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k+1},\omega)-f^{\star}

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\leq{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{w}}f(\theta_{k+1},\omega)-f^{\star}+\frac{1}{2\beta_{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm{dist}(A\theta_{k+1},\mathcal{K})\right]^{2}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}F_{\beta_{k}}(\theta_{k+1})-F^{\star}\leq 9^{\frac{1}{3}}\frac{C_{\delta}}{(k+8)^{\frac{1}{3}}}.

Now, we combine this with (
30
), and we get

\displaystyle-\|y^{\star}\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathrm{dist}(A\theta_{k+1},\mathcal{K})+\frac{1}{2\beta_{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm{dist}(A\theta_{k+1},\mathcal{K})\right]^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 9^{\frac{1}{3}}\frac{C_{\delta}}{(k+8)^{\frac{1}{3}}}

This is a second order inequality in terms of
{\mathbb{E}}\mathrm{dist}(A\theta_{k},\mathcal{K})
. Solving this inequality, we get

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\mathrm{dist}(A\theta_{k+1},\mathcal{K})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\beta_{0}\|y^{\star}\|}{\sqrt{k+8}}+\frac{2\sqrt{2\cdot 9^{\frac{1}{3}}C_{\delta}\beta_{0}}}{(k+8)^{\frac{5}{12}}}.

4
Linear Span: Matching Pursuit and Coordinate Descent

In this chapter, we consider minimizing a convex function over the linear span of a set. This problem template covers Coordinate Descent as a special case. We provide a unified tight analysis for this general case. The presented approach is based on
[
Loc+18b
]
and was developed in collaboration with Anant Raj, Sai Praneeth Karimireddy, Gunnar Rätsch, Bernhard Schölkopf, Sebastian U. Stich, and Martin Jaggi. The proof of the accelerated convergence of Matching Pursuit and Coordinate Descent was a four-hand collaboration between Francesco Locatello and Anant Raj. Francesco Locatello and Anant Raj contributed equally to this publication.

15
Problem Formulation

In this chapter we focus on the minimization of a convex smooth function over the linear span of a set:

\underset{\theta\in\lin(\mathcal{A})}{\text{minimize}}\quad f(\theta).

(31)

In this optimization template, we consider the following setting:

\triangleright\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{H}
is a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
with associated inner product
\big\langle x,\,y\big\rangle\,\forall\,x,y\in\mathcal{H}
and induced norm
\|x\|^{2}:=\big\langle x,\,x\big\rangle,

\forall\,x\in\mathcal{H}
.

\triangleright\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{H}
is a compact and symmetric set (the “set of atoms” or dictionary) in
\mathcal{H}
.

\triangleright\penalty\ \penalty\ f\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathbb{R}
is a convex and
L
-smooth (
L
-Lipschitz gradient in the finite dimensional case) function. If
\mathcal{H}
is an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space, then
f
is assumed to be Fréchet differentiable.

\triangleright
We specifically focus on projection-free algorithms that select at each iteration an element
z
from
\mathcal{A}
and update the iterate as
\theta_{k+1}=\theta_{k}+\gamma_{k}z_{k}
, where
\gamma_{k}
is a suitable chosen stepsize.

We consider two distinct specific oracles for the optimization algorithm:

(i) first-order oracle that returns a pair
(f(\theta),\nabla f(\theta))
given
\theta
.

(ii) a stochastic version of the first-order oracle that returns a pair
(f(\theta),\nabla_{z}f(\theta))
given
\theta
where
z
is a randomly sampled element of
\mathcal{A}
and
\nabla_{z}f(\theta)=\big\langle\nabla f(\theta),\,z\big\rangle
.

This formulation of the optimization template is interesting as if
\mathcal{A}=\l 1
, then
\lin(\mathcal{A})
corresponds to a euclidean space and the Matching Pursuit and Coordinate Descent algorithms coincide. This allows to draw a parallelism between the two algorithms and present a tight unified analysis of both in the steepest and random pursuit cases.

16
Affine Invariant Algorithms

First, let us review the definition of affine invariant algorithms (also see
[
LJJ13
]
):

Definition 6
.

An optimization method that is invariant under affine transformations of its domain is said to be affine invariant. Let
M:\hat{\mathcal{D}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
be a
surjective
linear or affine map, then the iterate sequence of an affine invariant optimization algorithm on
\min_{\theta\in\mathcal{D}}f(\theta)
and

\min_{\hat{\theta}\in\hat{\mathcal{D}}}\hat{f}(\hat{\theta})
for
\hat{f}(\hat{\theta}):=f(M\hat{\theta})

is the same in the sense that there is a correspondence between each step of the iterate sequences through
M
.

In order to develop an affine invariant algorithm, we rely on the atomic norm
\|x\|_{\mathcal{A}}:=\inf\{c>0\colon x\in c\cdot\conv(\mathcal{A})\}
(also known as the gauge function of
\conv(\mathcal{A})
), which is affine invariant. A visualization of the atomic norm is presented in Figure
5
.
We measure the smoothness of the objective using the atomic norm:

L_{\mathcal{A}}:=\!\!\!\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\lin(\mathcal{A})\\
y=x+\gamma z\\
\|z\|_{\mathcal{A}}=1,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\end{subarray}}\frac{2}{\gamma^{2}}\big[f(y)-f(x)-\big\langle\nabla f(x),\,y-x\big\rangle\big]\,.

This notion of curvature is inspired by the curvature constant of Frank-Wolfe and Matching Pursuit in
[
Jag13
,
Loc+17
]
. It combines the complexity of the function
f
as well as the set
\mathcal{A}
into a single constant that is affine invariant under transformations of our input problem (
31
).
From this definition we can easily derive an affine invariant version of the regular smoothness upper bound (
4
), this time using atomic norms:

\displaystyle f(y)\leq f(x)+\big\langle\nabla f(x),\,y-x\big\rangle+\frac{L_{\mathcal{A}}}{2}\|y-x\|_{\mathcal{A}}\,,

The affine-invariant MP Algorithm
8
simply replaces the new smoothness definition into Algorithm
6
.

Algorithm 8

Affine Invariant Generalized Matching Pursuit
[
Loc+18b
]

1:

init

\theta_{0}\in\lin(\mathcal{A})

2:

for

k=0\dots K

3:

Find
z_{k}:=(\text{Approx-})\textit{lmo}_{\mathcal{A}}(\nabla f(\theta_{k}))

4:

\theta_{k+1}=\theta_{k}-\frac{\big\langle\nabla f(\theta_{k}),\,z_{k}\big\rangle}{L_{\mathcal{A}}}z_{k}

5:

end for

17
Affine Invariant Rates

First, we remark that using
\nabla\hat{f}=M^{T}\nabla f
it is simple to show that Algorithm
8
is affine invariant:

\displaystyle M\hat{\theta}_{k+1}

\displaystyle=M\left(\hat{\theta}_{k}+\frac{\langle\nabla\hat{f}(\hat{\theta}_{k}),\hat{b}_{k}\rangle}{L_{\mathcal{A}}}\hat{b}_{k}\right)=M\hat{\theta}_{k}+\frac{\langle\nabla\hat{f}(\hat{\theta}_{k}),\hat{b}_{k}\rangle}{L_{\mathcal{A}}}M\hat{b}_{k}=\theta_{k}+\frac{\langle\nabla\hat{f}(\hat{\theta}_{k}),\hat{b}_{k}\rangle}{L_{\mathcal{A}}}b_{k}

\displaystyle=\theta_{k}+\frac{\langle M^{T}\nabla f(\theta_{k}),\hat{b}_{k}\rangle}{L_{\mathcal{A}}}b_{k}=\theta_{k}+\frac{\langle\nabla f(\theta_{k}),M\hat{b}_{k}\rangle}{L_{\mathcal{A}}}b_{k}=\theta_{k}+\frac{\langle\nabla f(\theta_{k}),b_{k}\rangle}{L_{\mathcal{A}}}b_{k}=\theta_{k+1}\,.

For the analysis, we call
\theta^{\star}
the minimizer of problem (
31
). If the optimum is not unique, we pick the one that yields worst-case constants.

17.1
Sublinear Rates for Convex Functions

In order to prove convergence, we start by defining the level set radius measured with the atomic norm as:

R_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}:=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta\in\lin(\mathcal{A})\\
f(\theta)\leq f(\theta_{0})\end{subarray}}\|\theta-\theta^{\star}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{A}}\,.

(32)

When we measure this radius with the
\|\cdot\|_{2}
we call it
R_{2}^{2}
, and when we measure it with
\|\cdot\|_{1}
we call it
R_{1}^{2}
.

Remark

Measuring smoothness using the atomic norm guarantees that:

Lemma 7
.

Assume
f
is
L
-smooth w.r.t. a given norm
\|\cdot\|
, over
\lin(\mathcal{A})
where
\mathcal{A}
is symmetric.
Then,

L_{\mathcal{A}}\leq L\,\radius_{\|\cdot\|}(\mathcal{A})^{2}\,.

(33)

As a motivating example, in coordinate descent, we will measure smoothness with the atomic norm being the L1-norm. Lemma
7
implies that
L_{\mathcal{A}}\leq L_{2}
where
L_{2}
is the smoothness constant measured with the L2-norm.

We are now ready to prove the convergence rate of Algorithm
8
for smooth convex functions. We only consider multiplicative errors for the
lmo
as in Equation (
lmo – multiplicative
).

Theorem 8
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{H}
be a closed and bounded set. We assume that
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{A}}
is a norm over
\lin(\mathcal{A})
. Let
f
be convex and
L_{\mathcal{A}}
-smooth w.r.t. the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{A}}
over
\lin(\mathcal{A})
, and let
R_{\mathcal{A}}
be the radius of the level set of
\theta_{0}
measured with the atomic norm.
Then, Algorithm
8
converges for
k\geq 0
as

f(\theta_{k+1})-f(\theta^{\star})\leq\frac{2L_{\mathcal{A}}R_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}}{\delta^{2}(k+2)}\,,

where
\delta\in(0,1]
is the relative accuracy parameter of the employed approximate lmo as in Equation (
lmo – multiplicative
).

Discussion

Our proof relies on the affine invariant definition of smoothness and level set radius and the properties of atomic norms. The key ingredient for the proof is to realize that the
lmo
solution can be used to compute the dual atomic norm of the gradient. Overall, the proof shares the spirit of the classical proof of steepest coordinate descent from
[
Nes12
]
except:

\triangleright
We do not assume orthogonal atoms and allow for potentially different L2-norms. Our atoms do not correspond to the natural basis of the ambient space.

\triangleright
We only assume that
\mathcal{A}
is closed, bounded and
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{A}}
is a norm over
\lin(\mathcal{A})
. Therefore,
\lin(\mathcal{A})
could be a subset of the ambient space.

\triangleright
We further do not make any incoherence assumption nor sparsity assumption. We support continuous atom sets
e.g.
when
\mathcal{A}
is the L2-ball, our algorithm and analysis perfectly recover gradient descent.

Comparison with Previous MP Rates

The analysis of our sublinear convergence rate is fundamentally different from the one proved in
[
Loc+17
]
. As their proof is inspired by the technique used for Frank-Wolfe by
[
Jag13
]
, they suffer from a dependency on the atomic norm of the whole iterate sequence
\rho:=\max\left\{\|\theta^{\star}\|_{\mathcal{A}},\|\theta_{0}\|_{\mathcal{A}}\ldots,\|\theta_{K}\|_{\mathcal{A}}\right\}<\infty
. Although their algorithm is also affine invariant, their notion of smoothness depends explicitly on
\rho
, which introduces a circular dependency if one wants to achieve a tight rate (otherwise, using an upper bound to the smoothness constant in the algorithm is sufficient). Our solution of redefining the smoothness constant with atomic norms is more elegant and requires a significantly simpler proof without any additional unnecessary assumption such as
\rho
being finite.

Comparison with Previous CD Rates

As a special case of our analysis we can read off the rate of steepest coordinate descent. We simply set
\mathcal{A}
to the L1-ball in an
n
dimensional space and obtain (in the case of exact
lmo
):

\displaystyle f(\theta_{k+1})-f(\theta^{\star})\leq\frac{2L_{1}R_{1}^{2}}{k+2}\leq\frac{2L_{2}R_{1}^{2}}{k+2}\leq\frac{2L_{2}nR_{2}^{2}}{k+2}\,,

where the first inequality is our rate, the second is
[
SRJ17
]
and the last is
[
Nes12
]
, both with global Lipschitz constant. Our rate for steepest coordinate descent is the tightest known with a global smoothness constant. Note that for coordinate-wise
L
our definition is equivalent to the classical one.
L_{\mathcal{A}}\leq L_{2}
if the norm is defined over more than one dimension (i.e. blocks), otherwise there is equality. For the relationship of
L_{1}
-smoothness to coordinate-wise smoothness, see also
[
Kar+19
, Theorem 4 in Appendix]
.

Affine Invariant Coordinate Descent?

Our approach is affine invariant as the basis becomes part of the definition of the optimization problem. If we transform the problem with an affine transformation
M
the atoms do not correspond to the natural coordinates anymore. The transformed coordinates are
\hat{e_{i}}=M^{-1}e_{i}
where
M^{-1}
is the inverse of the affine map. Although MP and CD coincide for one particular choice of basis, the latter algorithm is not affine invariant (unless one transforms the basis as in MP).

17.2
Intermezzo: Random Pursuit

[
Nes12
]
argued that steepest coordinate descent is at a disadvantage compared to random coordinate descent for large scale optimization problems. In the latter case, only a random coordinate of the gradient needs to be computed with no loss in terms of convergence rate. We can likewise extend our analysis to the
random pursuit
case where
z
is randomly sampled from a distribution over
\mathcal{A}
, rather than picked by a linear minimization oracle as in
[
SMG13
]
.
Assuming we can cheaply compute the projection of the gradient onto a single atom
\big\langle\nabla f,\,z\big\rangle
(for example, approximating it with finite differences), we can exploit the definition of the inexact oracle to give a rate in the case of arbitrary atom sets:

\hat{\delta}^{2}:=\min_{d\in\lin(\mathcal{A})}\frac{{\mathbb{E}}_{z\in\mathcal{A}}\big\langle d,\,z\big\rangle^{2}}{\|d\|_{\mathcal{A}*}^{2}}\,.

(34)

where
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{A}*}
is the dual norm of
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{A}}
.
This constant already appears in
[
Sti14
]
to measure the convergence of random pursuit (
\beta^{2}
in his notation).
Uniformly sampling the corners of the L1-ball, we have
\hat{\delta}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}
.
Our definition of
\hat{\delta}
allows obtaining a rate for any sampling scheme that ensures
\hat{\delta}^{2}\neq 0
.

We are now ready to present the sublinear convergence rate of random pursuit.

Theorem 9
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{H}
be a closed and bounded set. We assume that
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{A}}
is a norm. Let
f
be convex and
L_{\mathcal{A}}
-smooth w.r.t. the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{A}}
over
\lin(\mathcal{A})
and let
R_{\mathcal{A}}
be the radius of the level set of
\theta_{0}
measured with the atomic norm.
Then, Algorithm
8
converges for
k\geq 0
as

{\mathbb{E}}_{z}\big[f(\theta_{k+1})\big]-f(\theta^{\star})\leq\frac{2L_{\mathcal{A}}R_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}}{\hat{\delta}^{2}(k+2)}\,,

when the
lmo
is replaced with random sampling of
z
from a distribution over
\mathcal{A}
. The expectation
{\mathbb{E}}_{z}
is computed over this distribution.

Discussion

This approach is very general, as it allows to guarantee convergence for
any
sampling scheme and
any
set
\mathcal{A}
provided that
\hat{\delta}^{2}\neq 0
. In particular:

\triangleright
Replacing the line search step on the quadratic upper bound given by smoothness with line search on
f
, our technique gives a rate for gradient-free approaches.

\triangleright
While the worst case convergence of steepest and random coordinate descent is the same, the best case speed-up of steepest CD is
\Theta\mathopen{}\left(n\right)\mathclose{}
.

\triangleright
Examples of computation of
\hat{\delta}^{2}
can be found in
[
Sti14
, Section 4.2]
. In particular, if
z
is sampled from a spherical distribution as in
[
SMG13
]
,
\hat{\delta}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}
.

\triangleright
If the sampling distribution is preserved across affine transformations of the domain,
\hat{\delta}^{2}
is affine invariant.

17.3
Linear Rates for Strongly Convex Functions

For the linear rate we define an affine invariant notion of strong convexity:

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{A}}:=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\lin(\mathcal{A})\\
x\neq y\end{subarray}}\frac{2}{\|y-x\|_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}}D(y,x)\,.

where
D(y,x):=f(y)-f(x)-\big\langle\nabla f(x),\,y-x\big\rangle
.
We can give the linear rate of both matching and random pursuit.

Theorem 10
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{H}
be a closed and bounded set. We assume that
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{A}}
is a norm. Let
f
be
\mu_{\mathcal{A}}
-strongly convex and
L_{\mathcal{A}}
-smooth w.r.t. the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{A}}
, both over
\lin(\mathcal{A})
.
Then, Algorithm
8
converges for
k\geq 0
as

\varepsilon_{k+1}\leq\big(1-\delta^{2}\frac{\mu_{\mathcal{A}}}{L_{\mathcal{A}}}\big)\varepsilon_{k}\,.

where
\varepsilon_{k}:=f(\theta_{k})-f(\theta^{\star})
.
If the LMO direction is sampled randomly from
\mathcal{A}
, Algorithm
8
converges for
k\geq 0
as

{\mathbb{E}}_{z}\left[\varepsilon_{k+1}|\theta_{k}\right]\leq\big(1-\hat{\delta}^{2}\frac{\mu_{\mathcal{A}}}{L_{\mathcal{A}}}\big)\varepsilon_{k}\,.

Comparison with Previous MP Rates

Similarly to the sublinear rate, our new proof does not rely on
\rho
and is tighter than with any other norm choice.
Let us recall the notion of
minimal directional width
from
[
Loc+17
]
, which is how they measure the complexity of the atom set for a chosen norm:

\displaystyle\mdw:=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\in\lin(\mathcal{A})\\
d\neq 0\end{subarray}}\max_{z\in\mathcal{A}}\big\langle\frac{d}{\|d\|},\,z\big\rangle\,.

Our affine invariant definition for strong convexity relates to the
\mdw
as:

Lemma 11
.

Assume
f
is
\mu
-strongly convex w.r.t. a given norm
\|\cdot\|
over
\lin(\mathcal{A})
and
\mathcal{A}
is symmetric. Then:

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{A}}\geq\mdw^{2}\mu\,.

This lemma allows us to recover the rate of
[
Loc+17
]
when the norm is fixed.

Comparison with Previous CD Rates

Note that for CD we have that
\mdw=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}
. Our rate compares to previous work as:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{k+1}\leq\left(1-\frac{\mu_{1}}{L_{1}}\right)\varepsilon_{k}\leq\left(1-\frac{\mu_{1}}{L}\right)\varepsilon_{k}\leq\left(1-\frac{\mu}{nL}\right)\varepsilon_{k}\,,

where the first is our rate, the second is the rate of steepest CD
[
Nut+15
]
and the last is the one for random CD
[
Nes12
]
.

18
Accelerated Rates

Grounding of Our Approach

We consider the acceleration technique in
[
SMG13
]
which in turn is based on
[
LS13
]
. Their accelerated coordinate descent algorithm maintains two sequences of iterates
\theta
and
v
that are updated along randomly sampled coordinates. Considering general atoms instead, this proof technique would lead us to an accelerated rate for random pursuit. To accelerate
matching
pursuit we need to
decouple
the updates of
\theta
and
v
, using the steepest descent direction for
\theta
and a randomly sampled atom for
v
.
The possibility of decoupling the updates was first noted in
[
Sti14
, Corollary 6.4]
though its implications for accelerating greedy coordinate descent or matching pursuit were not explored.

Additional Assumptions

For the accelerated rate, we further assume:

\triangleright
That the linear space spanned by the atoms
\mathcal{A}
is finite dimensional.

\triangleright
That random atoms are only sampled on a non-symmetric version of
\mathcal{A}
with all the atoms in the same half space. Line search ensures that sampling either
z
or
-z
yields the same update.

\triangleright
For simplicity, we focus on an exact
lmo
.

Whether these assumptions are necessary or not remains an open question.

\triangleright
We make assumptions on the sampling distribution as explained in the next paragraph.

Whether these assumptions are necessary or not remains an open question.

Key Ingredients

The main difference between working with atoms and working with coordinates is that randomly sampling the coordinates of the gradient gives an unbiased estimate of the gradient itself in expectation. In other words, for any vector
d
we have:

{\mathbb{E}}_{i\in[n]}{\left[\big\langle e_{i},\,d\big\rangle e_{i}\Large\right]}=\frac{1}{n}d\,.

This is not true for general atom sets. To correct this issue, we morph the geometry of the space to achieve comparable sampling properties. The disadvantage of this approach is that it makes the proof dependent on a specific norm choice and therefore is not affine invariant.

Suppose we sample the atoms from a random variable
{\mathbf{z}}
defined over
\mathcal{A}
, we define

\tilde{P}:={\mathbb{E}}_{z\sim{\mathbf{z}}}[zz^{\top}]\,.

We assume that the distribution
{\mathbf{z}}
is such that
\lin(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\text{range}(\tilde{P})
.
Further let
P=\tilde{P}^{\dagger}
be the pseudo-inverse of
\tilde{P}
. Note that both
P
and
\tilde{P}
are positive semi-definite matrices. We can equip our space with a new inner product

\big\langle\cdot,\,P\cdot\big\rangle
and the resulting norm
\|\cdot\|_{P}
. With this new dot product, for each
d\in\lin(\mathcal{A})
we have:

{\mathbb{E}}_{z\sim{\mathbf{z}}}{\left[\big\langle z,\,Pd\big\rangle z\right]}={\mathbb{E}}_{z\sim{\mathbf{z}}}[zz^{\top}]Pd=P^{\dagger}Pd=d.

The last equality follows from our assumption that
\lin(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\text{range}(\tilde{P})
and
P^{\dagger}P
is an orthogonal projection operator onto the range of
\tilde{P}
.

The acceleration technique of
[
SMG13
]
works by optimizing two different quadratic subproblems at each iteration. The first is the regular smoothness upper bound. The second is a “model” of the function:

\displaystyle\psi_{k+1}(x)=\psi_{k}(x)+\alpha_{k+1}\Big(f(y_{k})+\langle{z}_{k}^{\top}\nabla f(y_{k}),{z}_{k}^{\top}P(x-y_{k})\rangle\Big)\,,

(35)

where

\psi_{0}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\|x-x_{0}\|_{P}^{2}
and
z
is sampled from
{\mathbf{z}}
.

18.1
Analysis

1:

init

\theta_{0}=v_{0}=y_{0}
,
\beta_{0}=0
, and
\nu

2:

for

k=0,1\dots K

3:

Solve
{\alpha_{k+1}^{2}}{L\nu}=\beta_{k}+\alpha_{k+1}

4:

\beta_{k+1}:=\beta_{k}+\alpha_{k+1}

5:

\tau_{k}:=\frac{\alpha_{k+1}}{\beta_{k+1}}

6:

Compute
y_{k}:=(1-\tau_{k})\theta_{k}+\tau_{k}v_{k}

7:

Find
z_{k}:=\textit{lmo}_{\mathcal{A}}(\nabla f(y_{k}))

8:

\theta_{k+1}:=y_{k}-\frac{\langle\nabla f(y_{k}),z_{k}\rangle}{L\|z_{k}\|_{2}^{2}}z_{k}

9:

Sample
\tilde{z}_{k}\sim{\mathbf{z}}

10:

v_{k+1}:=v_{k}-\alpha_{k+1}{\langle\nabla f(y_{k}),\tilde{z}_{k}\rangle}\tilde{z}_{k}

11:

end for

Algorithm 9

Accelerated Matching Pursuit
[
Loc+18b
]

1:

\ldots
as in Algorithm
9
, except replacing lines 7 to 10 by

2:

Sample
z_{k}\sim{\mathbf{z}}

3:

\theta_{k+1}:=y_{k}-\frac{\langle\nabla f(y_{k}),z_{k}\rangle}{L\|z_{k}\|^{2}_{2}}z_{k}

4:

v_{k+1}:=v_{k}-\alpha_{k+1}{\langle\nabla f(y_{k}),{z}_{k}\rangle}{z}_{k}

5:

end for

Algorithm 10

Accelerated Random Pursuit
[
Loc+18b
]

Lemma 12
.

The update of
v
in Algorithm
9
and
10
minimizes the model

v_{k}\in\argmin_{\theta}\psi_{k}(\theta)\,.

For both the algorithm and the analysis we need a constant
\nu
relating the geometry of the atom set with the sampling procedure (similar to
\hat{\delta}^{2}
in Equation (
34
)):

\nu\leq\max_{d\in\lin(\mathcal{A})}\frac{{\mathbb{E}}{\left[(\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}d)^{2}\|\tilde{z}_{k}\|_{P}^{2}\right]}\|{z(d)}\|_{2}^{2}}{(z(d)^{\top}d)^{2}}\,\quad\text{where}\quad z(d)=\textit{lmo}_{\mathcal{A}}(-d)\,.

Theorem 13
.

Let
f
be a convex function and
\mathcal{A}
be a symmetric compact set. Then the output of algorithm
9
for any
k\geq 1
converges with the following rate:

{\mathbb{E}}[f(\theta_{k})]-f(\theta^{\star})\leq\frac{2L\nu}{k(k+1)}\|\theta^{\star}-\theta_{0}\|^{2}_{P}\,.

From the rate of the greedy approach we can easily derive the rate for random pursuit:

Theorem 14
.

Let
f
be a convex function and
\mathcal{A}
be a symmetric set. Then the output of the algorithm
10
for any
k\geq 1
converges with the following rate:

{\mathbb{E}}[f(\theta_{k})]-f(\theta^{\star})\leq\frac{2L\nu^{\prime}}{k(k+1)}\|\theta^{\star}-\theta_{0}\|^{2}_{P}\,,\quad\text{where}\quad\nu^{\prime}\leq\max_{d\in\lin(\mathcal{A})}\frac{{\mathbb{E}}{\left[({z}_{k}^{\top}d)^{2}\|{z}_{k}\|_{P}^{2}\right]}}{{\mathbb{E}}{\left[({z}_{k}^{\top}d)^{2}/\|{z}_{k}\|_{2}^{2}\right]}}\,.

18.2
Accelerated Greedy Coordinate Descent.

From our accelerated rate of random and matching pursuit, we can read the rates of random and greedy coordinate descent respectively by setting
\mathcal{A}
to the usual
\l 1
and
{\mathbf{z}}
is uniformly distributed. In this case, algorithm
10
reduces to the accelerated randomized coordinate method (ACDM) of
[
LS13
,
NS17
]
. Instead, the accelerated MP algorithm yield a novel accelerated greedy coordinate descent method. The same rate was simultaneously (and independently) derived by
[
LFM18
]
, which was published at the same ICML conference. The rate of accelerated greedy coordinate descent is again, in the worst case, the same as random coordinate descent but can be faster up to a factor
\Theta\mathopen{}\left(n\right)\mathclose{}
:

Lemma 15
.

When
\mathcal{A}=\{e_{i},i\in[n]\}
and
{\mathbf{z}}
is a uniform distribution over
\mathcal{A}
, then
P=nI
,
\nu^{\prime}=n
and
\nu\in[1,n]
.

19
Proofs

19.1
Proof of Lemma
7

Proof.

Let
D(y,\theta):=f(y)-f(\theta)+\gamma\langle\nabla f(\theta),y-\theta\rangle

By the definition of smoothness of
f
w.r.t.
\|\cdot\|
,

D(y,\theta)\leq\frac{L}{2}\|y-\theta\|^{2}\,.

Hence, from the definition of
L_{\mathcal{A}}
,

\displaystyle L_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta,y\in\lin(\mathcal{A})\\
y=\theta+\gamma z\\
\|z\|_{\mathcal{A}}=1,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\end{subarray}}\frac{2}{\gamma^{2}}\frac{L}{2}\|y-\theta\|^{2}=L\sup_{z\ s.t.\|z\|_{\mathcal{A}}=1}\,\|z\|^{2}=L\,\radius_{\|\cdot\|}(\mathcal{A})^{2}\ .\qed

19.2
Proof of Theorem
8

Proof.

Recall that
\tilde{z}_{k}
is the atom selected in iteration
k
by the approximate
lmo
defined in (
lmo – multiplicative
). We start by upper-bounding
f
using the definition of
L_{\mathcal{A}}
as follows:

\displaystyle f(\theta_{k+1})

\displaystyle\leq\min_{\gamma\in\mathbb{R}}f(\theta_{k})+\gamma\langle\nabla f(\theta_{k}),\tilde{z}_{k}\rangle+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}L_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle\leq f(\theta_{k})-\frac{\langle\nabla f(\theta_{k}),\tilde{z}_{k}\rangle^{2}}{2L_{\mathcal{A}}}=f(\theta_{k})-\frac{\langle\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k}),\tilde{z}_{k}\rangle^{2}}{2L_{\mathcal{A}}}\leq f(\theta_{k})-\delta^{2}\frac{\langle\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k}),z_{k}\rangle^{2}}{2L_{\mathcal{A}}}\,.

Where
\nabla_{\parallel}f
is the parallel component of the gradient wrt the linear span of
\mathcal{A}
. Note that
\|d\|_{\mathcal{A}*}:=\sup\left\{\langle z,d\rangle,z\in\mathcal{A}\right\}
is the dual of the atomic norm.
Therefore, by definition:

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k}),z_{k}\rangle^{2}=\|-\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k})\|^{2}_{\mathcal{A}*}\,,

which gives:

\displaystyle f(\theta_{k+1})

\displaystyle\leq f(\theta_{k})-\delta^{2}\frac{1}{2L_{\mathcal{A}}}\|\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k})\|^{2}_{\mathcal{A}*}\leq f(\theta_{k})-\delta^{2}\frac{1}{2L_{\mathcal{A}}}\frac{\left(-\langle\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k}),\theta_{k}-\theta^{\star}\rangle\right)^{2}}{R_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}}

\displaystyle=f(\theta_{k})-\delta^{2}\frac{1}{2L_{\mathcal{A}}}\frac{\left(\langle\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k}),\theta_{k}-\theta^{\star}\rangle\right)^{2}}{R_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}}\leq f(\theta_{k})-\delta^{2}\frac{1}{2L_{\mathcal{A}}}\frac{\left(f(\theta_{k})-f(\theta^{\star})\right)^{2}}{R_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}}\,,

where the second inequality is Cauchy-Schwarz and the third one is convexity.
Which gives:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{k+1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2L_{\mathcal{A}}R_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}}{\delta^{2}(k+2)}\,.\qed

19.3
Proof of Theorem
9

Proof.

Recall that
\tilde{z}_{k}
is the atom selected in iteration
k
by the approximate
lmo
defined in (
lmo – multiplicative
). We start by upper-bounding
f
using the definition of
L_{\mathcal{A}}
as follows

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{z}f(\theta_{k+1})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{z}\left[\min_{\gamma\in\mathbb{R}}f(\theta_{k})+\gamma\langle\nabla f(\theta_{k}),z\rangle+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}L_{\mathcal{A}}\right]\leq f(\theta_{k})-\frac{{\mathbb{E}}_{z}\left[\langle\nabla f(\theta_{k}),z\rangle^{2}\right]}{2L_{\mathcal{A}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(\theta_{k})-\frac{{\mathbb{E}}_{z}\left[\langle\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k}),z\rangle^{2}\right]}{2L_{\mathcal{A}}}\leq f(\theta_{k})-\hat{\delta}^{2}\frac{\langle\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k}),z_{k}\rangle^{2}}{2L_{\mathcal{A}}}\,.

The rest of the proof proceeds as in Theorem
8
.
∎

19.4
Proof of Lemma
11

Proof.

First of all, note that for any
\theta,y\in\lin(\mathcal{A})
with
\theta\neq y
we have that:

\displaystyle\langle\nabla f(x),x-y\rangle^{2}\leq\|\nabla f(\theta)\|_{\mathcal{A}*}^{2}\|\theta-y\|_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}\,.

Therefore:

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta,y\in\lin\mathcal{A}\\
\theta\neq y\end{subarray}}\frac{2}{\|y-\theta\|_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}}D(\theta,y)\geq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta,y,d\in\lin\mathcal{A}\\
\theta\neq y,d\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{\|d\|_{\mathcal{A}*}^{2}}{\langle d,\theta-y\rangle^{2}}2D(\theta,y)

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta,y,d\in\lin\mathcal{A}\\
\theta\neq y,d\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{\|d\|_{\mathcal{A}*}^{2}}{\langle d,\theta-y\rangle^{2}}\mu\|\theta-y\|^{2}\geq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta,y,d\in\lin\mathcal{A}\\
\theta\neq y,d\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{\|d\|_{\mathcal{A}*}^{2}}{\langle d,\frac{\theta-y}{\|\theta-y\|}\rangle^{2}}\mu

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta,y,d\in\lin\mathcal{A}\\
\theta\neq y,d\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{\|d\|_{\mathcal{A}*}^{2}}{\|d\|^{2}}\mu\geq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\in\lin\mathcal{A}\\
d\neq 0\end{subarray}}\max_{z}\frac{\langle d,z\rangle^{2}}{\|d\|^{2}}\mu=\mdw^{2}\mu\,.\qed

19.5
Proof of Theorem
10

Proof.

(Part 1).

We start by upper-bounding
f
using the definition of
L_{\mathcal{A}}
as follows

\displaystyle f(\theta_{k+1})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\min_{\gamma\in\mathbb{R}}f(\theta_{k})+\gamma\langle\nabla f(\theta_{k}),\tilde{z}_{k}\rangle+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}L_{\mathcal{A}}\leq f(\theta_{k})-\frac{\langle\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k}),\tilde{z}_{k}\rangle^{2}}{2L_{\mathcal{A}}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle f(\theta_{k})-\delta^{2}\frac{\langle\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k}),z_{k}\rangle^{2}}{2L_{\mathcal{A}}}=f(\theta_{k})-\delta^{2}\frac{\langle-\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k}),z_{k}\rangle^{2}}{2L_{\mathcal{A}}}\,.

Where
\|d\|_{\mathcal{A}*}:=\sup\left\{\langle z,d\rangle,z\in\mathcal{A}\right\}
is the dual of the atomic norm.
Therefore, by definition:

\displaystyle\langle-\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k}),z_{k}\rangle^{2}=\|\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k})\|^{2}_{\mathcal{A}*}\,,

which gives
f(\theta_{k+1})\leq f(\theta_{k})-\delta^{2}\frac{1}{2L_{\mathcal{A}}}\|\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k})\|^{2}_{\mathcal{A}*}\,.

From strong convexity we have that:

\displaystyle f(y)\geq f(\theta)+\langle\nabla f(\theta),y-\theta\rangle+\frac{\mu_{\mathcal{A}}}{2}\|y-\theta\|_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}\,.

Fixing
y=\theta_{k}+\gamma(\theta^{\star}-\theta_{k})
and
\gamma=1
in the LHS and minimizing the RHS we obtain:

\displaystyle f(\theta^{\star})

\displaystyle\geq f(\theta_{k})-\frac{1}{2\mu_{\mathcal{A}}}\frac{\langle\nabla f(\theta_{k}),\theta^{\star}-\theta_{k}\rangle}{\|\theta^{\star}-\theta_{k}\|_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}}\geq f(\theta_{k})-\frac{1}{2\mu_{\mathcal{A}}}\|\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k})\|_{\mathcal{A}^{*}}^{2}\,,

where the last inequality is obtained by the fact that
\langle\nabla f(\theta_{k}),\theta^{\star}-\theta_{k}\rangle=\langle\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k}),\theta^{\star}-\theta_{k}\rangle
and Cauchy-Schwartz.
Therefore
\|\nabla f(\theta_{k})\|_{\mathcal{A}^{*}}\geq 2\varepsilon_{k}\mu_{\mathcal{A}}\,,

which yields

\varepsilon_{k+1}\leq\varepsilon_{k}-\delta^{2}\frac{\mu_{\mathcal{A}}}{L_{\mathcal{A}}}\varepsilon_{k}\,.

(Part 2).

We start by upper-bounding
f
using the definition of
L_{\mathcal{A}}
as follows

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{z}\left[f(\theta_{k+1})\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{z}\left[\min_{\gamma\in\mathbb{R}}f(\theta_{k})+\gamma\langle\nabla f(\theta_{k}),\tilde{z}_{k}\rangle+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}L_{\mathcal{A}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle f(\theta_{k})-{\mathbb{E}}_{z}\left[\frac{\langle\nabla f(\theta_{k}),\tilde{z}_{k}\rangle^{2}}{2L_{\mathcal{A}}}\right]\leq f(\theta_{k})-\hat{\delta}^{2}\frac{\langle\nabla f(\theta_{k}),z_{k}\rangle^{2}}{2L_{\mathcal{A}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(\theta_{k})-\hat{\delta}^{2}\frac{\langle\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k}),\tilde{z}_{k}\rangle^{2}}{2L_{\mathcal{A}}}=f(\theta_{k})-\hat{\delta}^{2}\frac{\langle-\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k}),\tilde{z}_{k}\rangle^{2}}{2L_{\mathcal{A}}}\,.

The rest of the proof proceeds as in Part 1 of the proof of Theorem
10
.
∎

19.6
Accelerated Matching Pursuit

Our proof follows the technique for acceleration from
[
LS13
,
NS17
,
Nes04
,
SMG13
]

We define
\|\theta\|^{2}_{P}=\theta^{\top}P\theta
. We start our proof by first defining the model function
\psi_{k}
. For
k=0
, we define:

\psi_{0}(\theta)=\frac{1}{2}\|\theta-v_{0}\|_{P}^{2}\,.

Then for
k>1
,
\psi_{k}
is inductively defined as

\displaystyle\psi_{k+1}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\psi_{k}(\theta)+\alpha_{k+1}\Big(f(y_{k})+\langle\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}\nabla f(y_{k}),\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}P(\theta-y_{k})\rangle\Big)\,.

(36)

Proof of Lemma
12
.

We will prove the statement inductively. For
k=0
,
\psi_{0}(\theta)=\frac{1}{2}\|\theta-v_{0}\|_{P}^{2}
and so the statement holds. Suppose it holds for some
k\geq 0
. Observe that the function
\psi_{k}(\theta)
is a quadratic with Hessian
P
. This means that we can reformulate
\psi_{k}(\theta)
with minima at
v_{k}
as

\psi_{k}(\theta)=\psi_{k}(v_{k})+\frac{1}{2}\|\theta-v_{k}\|_{P}^{2}\,.

Using this reformulation,

\displaystyle\argmin_{\theta}\psi_{k+1}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\theta}\Big\{\psi_{k}(\theta)+\alpha_{k+1}\Big(f(y_{k})+\langle\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}\nabla f(y_{k}),\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}P(\theta-y_{k})\rangle\Big)\Big\}

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\theta}\Big\{\psi_{k}(v_{k})+\frac{1}{2}\|\theta-v_{k}\|_{P}^{2}+\alpha_{k+1}\Big(f(y_{k})+\langle\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}\nabla f(y_{k}),\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}P(\theta-y_{k})\rangle\Big)\Big\}

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\theta}\Big\{\frac{1}{2}\|\theta-v_{k}\|_{P}^{2}+\alpha_{k+1}\langle\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}\nabla f(y_{k}),\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}P(\theta-v_{k})\rangle\Big\}

\displaystyle=v_{k}-\alpha_{k+1}{\langle\nabla f(y_{k}),\tilde{z}_{k}\rangle}\tilde{z}_{k}=v_{k+1}\,.\qed

Lemma 16

(Upper bound on
\psi_{k}(\theta)
)
.

{\mathbb{E}}[\psi_{k}(\theta)]\leq\beta_{k}f(\theta)+\psi_{0}(\theta)\,.

Proof.

We will also show this through induction. The statement is trivially true for
k=0
since
\beta_{0}=0
. Assuming the statement holds for some
k\geq 0
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\psi_{k+1}(\theta)]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\Big[\psi_{k}(\theta)+\alpha_{k+1}\Big(f(y_{k})+\langle\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}\nabla f(y_{k}),\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}P(\theta-y_{k})\rangle\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\Big[\psi_{k}(\theta)\Big]+\alpha_{k+1}{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\Big(f(y_{k})+\langle\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}\nabla f(y_{k}),\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}P(\theta-y_{k})\rangle\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\beta_{k}f(\theta)+\psi_{0}(\theta)+\alpha_{k+1}\Big(f(y_{k})+\nabla f(y_{k})^{\top}{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\tilde{z}_{k}\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}\Big]P(\theta-y_{k})\rangle\Big)

\displaystyle=\beta_{k}f(\theta)+\psi_{0}(\theta)+\alpha_{k+1}\Big(f(y_{k})+\nabla f(y_{k})^{\top}P^{\dagger}P(\theta-y_{k})\rangle\Big)

\displaystyle=\beta_{k}f(\theta)+\psi_{0}(\theta)+\alpha_{k+1}\Big(f(y_{k})+\nabla f(y_{k})^{\top}(\theta-y_{k})\rangle\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\beta_{k}f(\theta)+\psi_{0}(\theta)+\alpha_{k+1}f(\theta)\,.

In the above, we used the convexity of the function
f(\theta)
and the definition of
P
.
∎

Lemma 17

(Bound on progress)
.

For any
k\geq 0
of algorithm
9
,

f(\theta_{k+1})-f(y_{k})\leq-\frac{1}{2L\|z_{k}\|^{2}_{2}}\nabla f(y_{k})^{\top}{\left[z_{k}z_{k}^{\top}\right]}\nabla f(y_{k})\,.

Proof.

The update
\theta_{k+1}
along with the smoothness of
f(\theta)
guarantees that for
\gamma_{k+1}=\frac{\langle\nabla f(y_{k}),z_{k}\rangle}{L\|z_{k}\|^{2}}
,

\displaystyle f(\theta_{k+1})

\displaystyle=f(y_{k}+\gamma_{k+1}z_{k})\leq f(y_{k})+\gamma_{k+1}\big\langle\nabla f(y_{k}),\,z_{k}\big\rangle+\frac{L\gamma_{k+1}^{2}}{2}\|z_{k}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=f(y_{k})-\frac{1}{2L\|z_{k}\|^{2}_{2}}\nabla f(y_{k})^{\top}{\left[z_{k}z_{k}^{\top}\right]}\nabla f(y_{k})\,.

∎

Lemma 18

(Lower bound on
\psi_{k}(\theta)
)
.

Given a filtration
\mathcal{F}_{k}
upto time step
k
,

{\mathbb{E}}[\min_{\theta}\psi_{k}(\theta)|\mathcal{F}_{k}]\geq\beta_{k}f(\theta_{k})\,.

Proof.

This too we will show inductively. For
k=0
,
\psi_{k}(\theta)=\frac{1}{2}\|\theta-v_{0}\|_{P}^{2}\geq 0
with
\beta_{0}=0
. Assume the statement holds for some
k\geq 0
. Recall that
\psi_{k}(\theta)
has a minima at
v_{k}
and can be alternatively formulated as
\psi_{k}(v_{k})+\frac{1}{2}\|\theta-v_{k}\|_{P}^{2}
. Using this,

\displaystyle\psi_{k+1}^{\star}

\displaystyle=\min_{\theta}{\left[\psi_{k}(\theta)+\alpha_{k+1}{\left(\big\langle{\tilde{z}}_{k}^{\top}\nabla f(y_{k}),\,{\tilde{z}}_{k}^{\top}P(\theta-y_{k})\big\rangle+f(y_{k})\right)}\right]}

\displaystyle=\min_{\theta}{\left[\psi_{k}(v_{k})+\alpha_{k+1}{\left(\big\langle{\tilde{z}}_{k}^{\top}\nabla f(y_{k}),\,{\tilde{z}}_{k}^{\top}P(\theta-y_{k})\big\rangle+\frac{1}{2\alpha_{k+1}}\|\theta-v_{k}\|_{P}^{2}+f(y_{k})\right)}\right]}

\displaystyle=\psi_{k}^{\star}+\alpha_{k+1}f(y_{k})+\alpha_{k+1}\min_{\theta}{\left[\big\langle P{\tilde{z}}_{k}{\tilde{z}}_{k}^{\top}\nabla f(y_{k}),\,\theta-y_{k}\big\rangle+\frac{1}{2\alpha_{k+1}}\|\theta-v_{k}\|_{P}^{2}\right]}\,.

Since we defined
y_{k}=(1-\tau_{k})\theta_{k}+\tau_{k}v_{k}
, rearranging the terms gives us that

y_{k}-v_{k}=\frac{1-\tau_{k}}{\tau_{k}}(\theta_{k}-y_{k})\,.

Let us take now compute
{\mathbb{E}}[\psi_{k+1}^{\star}|\mathcal{F}_{k}]
by combining the above two equations:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\psi_{k+1}^{\star}|\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle=\psi_{k}^{\star}+\alpha_{k+1}f(y_{k})+\frac{\alpha_{k+1}(1-\tau_{k})}{\tau_{k}}\big\langle P{\mathbb{E}}_{k}[\tilde{z}_{k}\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}]\nabla f(y_{k}),\,y_{k}-\theta_{k}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\alpha_{k+1}{\mathbb{E}}_{k}\min_{\theta}{\left[\big\langle P\tilde{z}_{k}\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}\nabla f(y_{k}),\,\theta-v_{k}\big\rangle+\frac{1}{2\alpha_{k+1}}\|\theta-v_{k}\|_{P}^{2}\right]}

\displaystyle=\psi_{k}^{\star}+\alpha_{k+1}f(y_{k})+\frac{\alpha_{k+1}(1-\tau_{k})}{\tau_{k}}\big\langle\nabla f(y_{k}),\,y_{k}-\theta_{k}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\alpha_{k+1}{\mathbb{E}}_{k}\min_{\theta}{\left[\big\langle P\tilde{z}_{k}\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}\nabla f(y_{k}),\,\theta-v_{k}\big\rangle+\frac{1}{2\alpha_{k+1}}\|\theta-v_{k}\|_{P}^{2}\right]}

\displaystyle=\psi_{k}^{\star}+\alpha_{k+1}f(y_{k})+\frac{\alpha_{k+1}(1-\tau_{k})}{\tau_{k}}\big\langle\nabla f(y_{k}),\,y_{k}-\theta_{k}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt-\frac{\alpha_{k+1}^{2}}{2}\nabla f(y_{k})^{\top}{\mathbb{E}}_{k}{\left[\tilde{z}_{k}\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}PP^{-1}P\tilde{z}_{k}\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}\right]}\nabla f(y_{k})

\displaystyle=\psi_{k}^{\star}+\alpha_{k+1}f(y_{k})+\frac{\alpha_{k+1}(1-\tau_{k})}{\tau_{k}}\big\langle\nabla f(y_{k}),\,y_{k}-\theta_{k}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt-\frac{\alpha_{k+1}^{2}}{2}\nabla f(y_{k})^{\top}{\mathbb{E}}_{k}{\left[\tilde{z}_{k}\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}P\tilde{z}_{k}\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}\right]}\nabla f(y_{k})\,.

Let us define a constant
\nu\geq 0
such that it is the smallest number for which the below inequality holds for all
k
,

\nu\nabla f(y_{k})^{\top}\frac{{\left[z_{k}z_{k}^{\top}\right]}}{2L\|z_{k}\|^{2}_{2}}\nabla f(y_{k})\geq\nabla f(y_{k})^{\top}{\mathbb{E}}{\left[\tilde{z}_{k}\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}P\tilde{z}_{k}\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}\right]}\nabla f(y_{k})\,.

Also recall from Lemma
17
that

f(\theta_{k+1})-f(y_{k})\leq-\frac{1}{2L\|z_{k}\|^{2}_{2}}\nabla f(y_{k})^{\top}{\left[z_{k}z_{k}^{\top}\right]}\nabla f(y_{k})\,.

Using the above two statements in our computation of
\psi_{k+1}^{\star}
, we get

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\psi_{k+1}^{\star}|\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle=\psi_{k}^{\star}+\alpha_{k+1}f(y_{k})+\frac{\alpha_{k+1}(1-\tau_{k})}{\tau_{k}}\big\langle\nabla f(y_{k}),\,y_{k}-\theta_{k}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt-\frac{\alpha_{k+1}^{2}}{2}\nabla f(y_{k})^{\top}{\mathbb{E}}_{k}{\left[\tilde{z}_{k}\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}P\tilde{z}_{k}\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}\right]}\nabla f(y_{k})

\displaystyle\geq\psi_{k}^{\star}+\alpha_{k+1}f(y_{k})+\frac{\alpha_{k+1}(1-\tau_{k})}{\tau_{k}}\big\langle\nabla f(y_{k}),\,y_{k}-\theta_{k}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt-\frac{\alpha_{k+1}^{2}\nu}{2}\nabla f(y_{k})^{\top}{\left[z_{k}z_{k}^{\top}\right]}\nabla f(y_{k})

\displaystyle\geq\psi_{k}^{\star}+\alpha_{k+1}f(y_{k})+\frac{\alpha_{k+1}(1-\tau_{k})}{\tau_{k}}\big\langle\nabla f(y_{k}),\,y_{k}-\theta_{k}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+{\alpha_{k+1}^{2}L\nu}(f(\theta_{k+1})-f(y_{k}))

\displaystyle\geq\psi_{k}^{\star}+\alpha_{k+1}f(y_{k})+\frac{\alpha_{k+1}(1-\tau_{k})}{\tau_{k}}(f(y_{k})-f(\theta_{k}))

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+{\alpha_{k+1}^{2}L\nu}(f(\theta_{k+1})-f(y_{k}))\,.

Let us pick
\alpha_{k+1}
such that it satisfies
{\alpha_{k+1}^{2}}{\nu L}=\beta_{k+1}
. Then the above equation simplifies to

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\psi_{k+1}^{\star}|\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle\geq\psi_{k}^{\star}+\frac{\alpha_{k+1}}{\tau_{k}}f(y_{k})-\frac{\alpha_{k+1}(1-\tau_{k})}{\tau_{k}}f(\theta_{k})+\beta_{k+1}(f(\theta_{k+1})-f(y_{k}))

\displaystyle=\psi_{k}^{\star}-\beta_{k}f(\theta_{k})+\beta_{k+1}f(y_{k})-\beta_{k+1}f(y_{k})+\beta_{k+1}f(\theta_{k+1})

\displaystyle=\psi_{k}^{\star}-\beta_{k}f(\theta_{k})+\beta_{k+1}f(\theta_{k+1})\,.

We used that
\tau_{k}=\alpha_{k+1}/\beta_{k+1}
. Finally we use the inductive hypothesis to conclude that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\psi_{k+1}^{\star}|\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle\geq\psi_{k}^{\star}-\beta_{k}f(\theta_{k})+\beta_{k+1}f(\theta_{k+1})\geq\beta_{k+1}f(\theta_{k+1})\,.\qed

Lemma 19

(Final convergence rate)
.

For any
k\geq 1
the output of algorithm
9
satisfies:

{\mathbb{E}}[f(\theta_{k})]-f(\theta^{\star})\leq\frac{2L\nu}{k(k+1)}\|\theta^{\star}-\theta_{0}\|^{2}_{P}\,.

Proof.

Putting together Lemmas
16
and
18
, we have that

\beta_{k}{\mathbb{E}}[f(\theta_{k})]\leq{\mathbb{E}}[\psi_{k}^{\star}]\leq{\mathbb{E}}[\psi_{k}(\theta^{\star})]\leq\beta_{k}f(\theta^{\star})+\psi_{0}(\theta^{\star})\,.

Rearranging the terms we get

{\mathbb{E}}[f(\theta_{k})]-f(\theta^{\star})\leq\frac{1}{2\beta_{k}}\|\theta^{\star}-\theta_{0}\|^{2}_{P}\,.

To finish the proof of the theorem, we only have to compute the value of
\beta_{k}
.
Recall that

{\alpha_{k+1}^{2}}{L\nu}=\beta_{k}+\alpha_{k+1}\,.

We will inductively show that
\alpha_{k}\geq\frac{k}{2L\nu}
. For
k=0
,
\beta_{0}=0
and
\alpha_{1}=\frac{1}{2L\nu}
which satisfies the condition. Suppose that for some
k\geq 0
, the inequality holds for all iterations
i\leq k
. Recall that
\beta_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\alpha_{i}
i.e.
\beta_{k}\geq\frac{k(k+1)}{4L\nu}
. Then

\displaystyle(\alpha_{k+1}L\nu)^{2}-\alpha_{k+1}L\nu=\beta_{k}L\nu\geq\frac{k(k+1)}{4}\,.

The positive root of the quadratic
x^{2}-x-c=0
for
c\geq 0
is
x=\frac{1}{2}{\left(1+\sqrt{4c+1}\right)}
. Thus

\alpha_{k+1}L\nu\geq\frac{1}{2}{\left(1+\sqrt{k(k+1)+1}\right)}\geq\frac{k+1}{2}\,.

This finishes our induction and proves the final rate of convergence.
∎

Lemma 20

(Understanding
\nu
)
.

\nu\leq\max_{d\in\lin(\mathcal{A})}\frac{{\mathbb{E}}{\left[(\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}d)^{2}\|\tilde{z}_{k}\|_{P}^{2}\right]}\|{z(d)}\|_{2}^{2}}{(z(d)^{\top}d)^{2}}\,,

Proof.

Recall the definition of
\nu
as a constant which satisfies the following inequality for all iterations
k

\nu\nabla f(y_{k})^{\top}\frac{{\left[z_{k}z_{k}^{\top}\right]}}{2L\|z_{k}\|^{2}_{2}}\nabla f(y_{k})\geq\nabla f(y_{k})^{\top}{\mathbb{E}}{\left[\tilde{z}_{k}\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}P\tilde{z}_{k}\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}\right]}\nabla f(y_{k})\,.

which then yields the following sufficient condition for
\nu
:

\nu\leq\max_{d\in\lin(\mathcal{A})}\frac{{\mathbb{E}}{\left[(\tilde{z}_{k}^{\top}d)^{2}\|\tilde{z}_{k}\|_{P}^{2}\right]}\|{z(d)}\|_{2}^{2}}{(z(d)^{\top}d)^{2}}\,,

where
z(d)
is defined to be

z(d)=\textit{lmo}_{\mathcal{A}}(-d)\,.

∎

Proof of Theorem
14

The proof of Theorem
14
is exactly the same as that of the previous except that now the update to
v_{k}
is also a random variable. The only change needed is the definition of
\nu^{\prime}
where we need the following to hold:

\nu^{\prime}\nabla f(y_{k})^{\top}\frac{1}{2L}{\mathbb{E}}_{k}{\left[z_{k}z_{k}^{\top}/{\|z_{k}\|^{2}_{2}}\right]}\nabla f(y_{k})\geq\nabla f(y_{k})^{\top}{\mathbb{E}}{\left[{z}_{k}{z}_{k}^{\top}P{z}_{k}{z}_{k}^{\top}\right]}\nabla f(y_{k})\,.

Proof of Lemma
15

When
\mathcal{A}=\{e_{i},i\in[n]\}
and
\mathcal{Z}
has a uniform distribution over
\mathcal{A}
, then
\tilde{P}=1/nI
and
P=nI
. A simple computation shows that
\nu^{\prime}=n
and
\nu\in[1,n]
. Note that here
\nu
could be upto
n
times smaller than
\nu^{\prime}
meaning that our accelerated greedy coordinate descent algorithm could be
\sqrt{n}
times faster than the accelerated random coordinate descent. In the worst case
\nu=\nu^{\prime}
, but in practice one can pick a smaller
\nu
compared to
\nu^{\prime}
as the worst case gradient rarely happen. It is possible to tune
\nu
and
\nu^{\prime}
empirically but we do not explore this direction.

5
Convex Cones: Non-Negative Matching Pursuit

In this chapter, we consider the problem of minimizing a convex function over convex cones. The presented approach is based on
[
Loc+17a
]
and was developed in collaboration with Michael Tschannen, Gunnar Rätsch, and Martin Jaggi. In this dissertation, we present a new unpublished proof for the sublinear rate. The new proof is shorter and more elegant than the one in
[
Loc+17a
]
despite the rate being essentially the same.

20
Problem Formulation

In this chapter we study greedy projection-free optimization of smooth convex function over convex cones:

\underset{\theta\in\cone{A}}{\text{minimize}}\quad f(\theta).

(37)

We consider the following setting:

\triangleright\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{H}
is a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
with associated inner product
\big\langle x,\,y\big\rangle\,\forall\,x,y\in\mathcal{H}
and induced norm
\|x\|^{2}:=\big\langle x,\,x\big\rangle,

\forall\,x\in\mathcal{H}
.

\triangleright\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{H}
is a compact set (the “set of atoms” or dictionary) in
\mathcal{H}
. As opposed to chapter
4
, we do not assume that
\mathcal{A}
is symmetric.

\triangleright
Without loss of generality, we point the cone to the origin and assume that
0\in\mathcal{A}
(see next paragraph).

\triangleright\penalty\ \penalty\ f\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathbb{R}
is a convex and
L
-smooth (
L
-Lipschitz gradient in the finite dimensional case) function. If
\mathcal{H}
is an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space, then
f
is assumed to be Fréchet differentiable.

\triangleright
We specifically focus on projection-free algorithms that maintain the iterate as a non-negative linear combination of elements from
\mathcal{A}
.

Convex Cone

The cone
\cone(\mathcal{A}-y)
tangent to the convex set
\conv(\mathcal{A})
at a point
y
is formed by the half-lines emanating from
y
and intersecting
\conv(\mathcal{A})
in at least one other point.
Without loss of generality we consider
0\in\mathcal{A}
and consider the set
\cone(\mathcal{A})
(
i.e.
with
y=0
) to be closed. If
\mathcal{A}
is finite, the cone constraint can be written as

\cone(\mathcal{A}):=\{x:x=\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{A}|}\alpha_{i}z_{i}\;\;\text{s.t.}\;z_{i}\in\mathcal{A},\ \alpha_{i}\geq 0\penalty\ \forall i\}\!.

This setting is theoretically interesting as it represents an intermediate case between Frank-Wolfe and Matching Pursuit. Non-negative combinations of atoms are very natural in some applications
[
ELX13
,
GL17
,
Beh+13
,
MS11
,
Ber+07
,
KP12
]
. On the other hand, the convergence properties of these heuristic algorithms remain unclear.

21
A Simple Non-Negative MP Method

First, we present a simple method in Algorithm
11
and its convergence on general convex functions. Unfortunately, this method does not enjoy a linear rate for strongly convex objectives. We will fix this issue using corrective variants in Section
22
. Note that the algorithm definition is not affine invariant but can be made so replacing
L\|z_{k}\|^{2}
by
L_{\cone(\mathcal{A})}
defined as in Equation (
39
).

The main difference between Algorithm
11
and the general greedy template of Algorithm
1
is how we query the
lmo
. We do not only look for the steepest descent direction in
\mathcal{A}
but also add an iteration-dependent atom
-\frac{\theta_{k}}{\|\theta_{k}\|_{\mathcal{A}}}
to the set of possible search directions. Note that this only makes sense when
\theta_{k}\neq 0
; in that case, no direction is added. Further, we stress that
\|y\|_{\mathcal{A}}
is only well defined if
y\in\cone(\mathcal{A})
. Since the set
\mathcal{A}
contains the origin on a corner,
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{A}}
is not a valid norm in general.
Intuitively, one can think that if the
lmo
selects an atom in
\mathcal{A}
, the update will always increase its weight. Otherwise, it will shrink all non-zero weights taking a step towards the origin.

Algorithm 11

Non-Negative Matching Pursuit
[
Loc+17a
]

1:

init

\theta_{0}=0\in\mathcal{A}

2:

for

k=0\dots K

3:

Find
\bar{z}_{k}:=(\text{Approx-})\textit{lmo}_{\mathcal{A}}(\nabla f(\theta_{k}))

4:

z_{k}=\argmin_{z\in\left\{\bar{z}_{k},\frac{-\theta_{k}}{\|\theta_{k}\|_{\mathcal{A}}}\right\}}\langle\nabla f(\theta_{k}),z\rangle

5:

\gamma:=\frac{\langle-\nabla f(\theta_{k}),z_{k}\rangle}{L\|z_{k}\|^{2}}

6:

Update
\theta_{k+1}:=\theta_{k}+\gamma z_{k}

7:

end for

Recall the alignment assumption from
[
PR19
]
discussed in Section
7
. Our variation on the
lmo
follows this assumption. In fact, if
\theta_{k}
is not an optimum of the optimization problem in Equation (
37
) and if
\min_{z\in\mathcal{A}}\langle\nabla f(\theta_{k}),z\rangle=0
, the vector
-\frac{\theta_{k}}{\|\theta_{k}\|_{\mathcal{A}}}
is aligned with
\nabla f(\theta_{k})
in the sense that
\langle\nabla f(\theta_{k}),-\frac{\theta_{k}}{\|\theta_{k}\|_{\mathcal{A}}}\rangle<0
. This effectively ensure that the algorithm does not stop unless
\theta_{k}
is a solution of the optimization problem.

Formally, we define the set of feasible descent directions of Algorithm
11
at a point
\theta\in\cone(\mathcal{A})
as:

T_{\mathcal{A}}(\theta):=\left\{d\in\mathcal{H}\!:\exists z\in\mathcal{A}\cup\Big\{-\frac{\theta}{\|\theta\|_{\mathcal{A}}}\Big\}\,\ \text{s.t.}\;\langle d,z\rangle<0\right\}.

(38)

Intuitively,
T_{\mathcal{A}}(\theta)
is the set of all possible directions for which the Algorithm would not terminate if the iterate were
\theta
.
If at some iteration
k
the gradient
\nabla f(\theta_{k})
is not in
T_{\mathcal{A}}(\theta_{k})
, then Algorithm
11
terminates as
\min_{z\in\mathcal{A}}\langle d,z\rangle=0
and
\langle d,-\theta_{k}\rangle\geq 0
(which yields
z_{k}=0
). This is expected as in that case
\theta_{k}

would be optimal.

Lemma 21
.

If
\tilde{\theta}\in\cone(\mathcal{A})
and
\nabla f(\tilde{\theta})\not\in T_{\mathcal{A}}
then
\tilde{\theta}
is a solution to
\min_{\theta\in\cone(\mathcal{A})}f(\theta)
.

Proof.

By contradiction, assume
\theta^{\star}\neq\tilde{\theta}
and
\nabla f(\tilde{\theta})\not\in T_{\mathcal{A}}
. Now, by convexity of
f
we have:

\displaystyle f(\theta^{\star})\geq f(\tilde{\theta})+\langle\nabla f(\tilde{\theta}),\theta^{\star}-\tilde{\theta}\rangle

Since
\theta^{\star}\neq\tilde{\theta}
we have also that
f(\theta^{\star})<f(\tilde{\theta})
. Therefore:

\displaystyle 0<f(\tilde{\theta})-f(\theta^{\star})\leq\langle-\nabla f(\tilde{\theta}),\theta^{\star}-\tilde{\theta}\rangle

which we rewrite as
\langle\nabla f(\tilde{\theta}),\theta^{\star}\rangle+\langle\nabla f(\tilde{\theta}),-\tilde{\theta}\rangle<0
. Now we note that
by the assumption that
\nabla f(\tilde{\theta})\not\in T_{\mathcal{A}}
we have that both these inner products are non negative which is absurd.
To draw this conclusion note that
\theta^{\star}\in\cone(\mathcal{A})
we have that
\theta^{\star}=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}z_{i}
where
z_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
and
\alpha_{i}\geq 0\ \forall\ i
.
∎

Notably,
\theta
remains feasible since
\theta_{0}=0
. The reason is that we compute
\gamma
minimizing the quadratic upper bound on
f
given by smoothness. Since
f
is convex, for
k>0
we have
f(\theta_{k})\leq f(0)
. Hence, the minimum of the upper bound lies in between
\theta_{k}
and the origin.

For the analysis, let

R_{\cone(\mathcal{A})}^{2}:=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta\in\cone(\mathcal{A})\\
f(\theta)\leq f(\theta_{0})\end{subarray}}\|\theta-\theta^{\star}\|^{2}_{\amxnok}

and

L_{\cone(\mathcal{A})}:=\!\!\!\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta,y\in\cone(\mathcal{A})\\
y=\theta+\gamma z\\
\|z\|_{\amxnok}=1,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\end{subarray}}\frac{2}{\gamma^{2}}\big[f(y)-f(\theta)-\big\langle\nabla f(\theta),\,y-\theta\big\rangle\big]\,.

(39)

Note that when
\theta=0
the norm
\|y\|_{\mathcal{A}}
is well defined only for all
y\in\cone(\mathcal{A})
and
y\neq 0
(see Figure
5
). This is not an issue because
\theta_{0}=0
.

Theorem 22
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{H}
be a bounded set,
f
be convex and
L_{\cone(\mathcal{A})}
smooth and let
R_{\cone(\mathcal{A})}
be the radius of the level set of
\theta_{0}
both measured with the atomic norm.
Then, Algorithm
11
converges for
k\geq 1
as

\varepsilon_{k+1}\leq\frac{2L_{\cone(\mathcal{A})}R_{\cone(\mathcal{A})}^{2}}{\delta^{2}(k+2)},

where
\delta\in(0,1]
is the relative accuracy parameter of the employed approximate lmo in Equation (
lmo – multiplicative
).

As opposed to other theorems, we report the full proof in this main section as it is a unpublished, new, and independent proof compared to the one in
[
Loc+17a
]
.

Proof.

Recall that
\tilde{z}_{k}\in\mathcal{A}\cup\left\{-\frac{\theta_{k}}{\|\theta_{k}\|_{\mathcal{A}}}\right\}
is the atom selected in iteration
k
by the approximate
lmo
. This ensures that the search direction is always negatively correlated with the gradient and hence is a descent direction and the algorithm does not stop unless at the constrained minimum. Furthermore, the quadratic upper bound holds also if
z_{k}=-\frac{\theta_{k}}{\|\theta_{k}\|_{\mathcal{A}}}
as it has atomic norm of one.
We start by upper-bounding
f
using the definition of
L_{\cone(\mathcal{A})}
:

\displaystyle f(\theta_{k+1})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\min_{\gamma\in\mathbb{R}}f(\theta_{k})+\gamma\langle\nabla f(\theta_{k}),\tilde{z}_{k}\rangle+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}L_{\cone(\mathcal{A})}

(40)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle f(\theta_{k})-\frac{\langle\nabla f(\theta_{k}),\tilde{z}_{k}\rangle^{2}}{2L_{cone(\mathcal{A})}}=f(\theta_{k})-\frac{\langle\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k}),\tilde{z}_{k}\rangle^{2}}{2L_{\cone(\mathcal{A})}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle f(\theta_{k})-\delta^{2}\frac{\langle\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k}),\tilde{z}_{k}\rangle^{2}}{2L_{\cone(\mathcal{A})}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(\theta_{k})-\delta^{2}\frac{1}{2L_{\cone(\mathcal{A})}}\|\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k})\|^{2}_{\amx*}

Where we used that
\|d\|_{\amx*}:=\sup\left\{\langle z,d\rangle,z\in\amx\right\}
is the dual of the atomic norm.
Using Lemma
21
, it is easy to show that:

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k})\|^{2}_{\amx*}

\displaystyle=\|\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k})-\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta^{\star})\|^{2}_{\amx*}

which in turn yields:

\displaystyle f(\theta_{k+1})

\displaystyle\leq f(\theta_{k})-\delta^{2}\frac{1}{2L_{\cone(\mathcal{A})}}\|\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k})-\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta^{\star})\|^{2}_{\amx*}

\displaystyle\leq f(\theta_{k})-\delta^{2}\frac{1}{2L_{\cone(\mathcal{A})}}\frac{\langle\nabla_{\parallel}f(\theta_{k}),\theta_{k}-\theta^{\star}\rangle^{2}}{R_{\cone(\mathcal{A})}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq f(\theta_{k})-\delta^{2}\frac{1}{2L_{\cone(\mathcal{A})}}\frac{\left(f(\theta_{k})-f(\theta^{\star})\right)^{2}}{R_{\cone(\mathcal{A})}^{2}}

Which gives the rate after solving the recursion:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{k+1}\leq\frac{2L_{\cone(\mathcal{A})}R_{\cone(\mathcal{A})}^{2}}{\delta^{2}(k+2)}

∎

Difference form the proof in
[
Loc+17a
]

In this dissertation, we presented a new proof for the sublinear rate of Algorithm
11
. This proof is significantly simpler than the one in the original publication and allows for an affine invariant algorithm without the circular dependency in the smoothness definition described in Chapter
4
. The original proof separates the cases where the weights increase or decrease, which the new proof avoids using the properties of atomic norms.

Limitations of Algorithm
11
:

Let us call
active
the atoms which have nonzero weights in the representation of
\theta_{k}=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\alpha_{i}z_{i}
computed by Algorithm
11
. Formally, the set of active atoms is defined as
\mathcal{S}:=\{z_{i}\colon\alpha_{i}>0,i=0,1,\ldots,k-1\}
.
The main drawback of Algorithm
11
is that when the direction
-\frac{\theta_{k}}{\|\theta_{k}\|_{\mathcal{A}}}
is selected, the weight of
all
active atoms is reduced.
This uniform reduction can lead the algorithm to alternately select
-\frac{\theta_{k}}{\|\theta_{k}\|_{\mathcal{A}}}
and an atom from
\mathcal{A}
, thereby slowing down convergence in a similar manner as the
zig-zagging
phenomenon well-known in the Frank-Wolfe framework
[
LJJ15
]
. A visualization of this issue is presented in Figure
4
(left).

22
Corrective Variants

To achieve linear convergence in the strongly convex case, we introduce corrective variants of Algorithm
11
that are inspired by corresponding approaches in FW
[
FW+56
,
LJJ15
]
. Our algorithms are the Away-steps Non-Negative MP (ANNMP) and Pairwise Non-Negative MP (PWNNMP), presented in Algorithm
12
and the fully-corrective Non-Negative MP (FCNNMP) in Algorithm
13
.Now, we query the
lmo
a second time on the active set
\mathcal{S}
(the set of atoms with non-zero weight in the iterate) to find the direction of steepest
ascent
. We use this information to selectively “reduce” the weight on this atom or “swap” it with the steepest descent direction. A visualization of the away step is presented in Figure
4
(right).

Figure 4
:
Visualization of the
zig-zagging
phenomenon of the NNMP. As the vanilla algorithm cannot selectively reduce the weights of the iterate it takes more step to reach the optimum. Instead, the ANNMP allows to take an away-step that selectively reduce the weight of an atom (the green atom in the visualization) which leads to faster convergence.

At each iteration, Algorithm
12
updates the weights of
z_{k}
and
v_{k}
as
\alpha_{z_{k}}=\alpha_{z_{k}}+\gamma
and
\alpha_{v_{k}}=\alpha_{v_{k}}-\gamma
, respectively. To ensure that
\theta_{k+1}\in\cone(\mathcal{A})
,
\gamma
has to be clipped according to the weight which is currently on
v_{k}
, i.e.,
\gamma_{\max}=\alpha_{v_{k}}
. If
\gamma=\gamma_{\max}
, we set
\alpha_{v_{k}}=0
and remove
v_{k}
from
\mathcal{S}
as the atom
v_{k}
is no longer active. If
d_{k}\in\mathcal{A}
(i.e., we take a regular MP step and not an away step), the line search is unconstrained (i.e.,
\gamma_{\max}=\infty
).
Note that while the second
lmo
query increases the computational complexity, an exact search may. be feasible in practice as at most
k
atoms can be active at iteration
k
.
Taking an additional computational burden, one can update the weights of all active atoms in the spirit of Orthogonal Matching Pursuit
[
MZ93
]
. This approach is implemented in the Fully Corrective MP (FCNNMP), Algorithm
13
.

Algorithm 12

Away-steps (ANNMP) and Pairwise (PWNNMP) Non-Negative MP
[
Loc+17a
]

1:

init

\theta_{0}=0\in\mathcal{A}
, and
\mathcal{S}:=\{\theta_{0}\}

2:

for

k=0\dots K

3:

Find
z_{k}:=(\text{Approx-})\textit{lmo}_{\mathcal{A}}(\nabla f(\theta_{k}))

4:

Find
v_{k}:=(\text{Approx-})\textit{lmo}_{\mathcal{S}}(-\nabla f(\theta_{k}))

5:

\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}\cup z_{k}

6:

ANNMP:

d_{k}\!=\!\argmin_{d\in\{z_{k},-v_{k}\}}\!\langle\nabla f(\theta_{k}),d\rangle\!

7:

PWNNMP:

d_{k}=z_{k}-v_{k}

8:

\gamma:=\min\left\{\frac{\langle-\nabla f(\theta_{k}),d_{k}\rangle}{L\|d_{k}\|^{2}},\gamma_{\max}\right\}
(
\gamma_{\max}
see text)

9:

Update
\alpha_{z_{k}}
,
\alpha_{v_{k}}
and
\mathcal{S}
according to
\gamma
(
\gamma
see text)

10:

Update
\theta_{k+1}:=\theta_{k}+\gamma d_{k}

11:

end for

Algorithm 13

Fully Corrective Non-Negative Matching Pursuit (FCNNMP)
[
Loc+17a
]

1:

…as Algorithm
12
, except replacing lines 6-10 with

2:

Variant 0 (norm corrective):

\theta_{k+1}\!=\!\underset{{\theta\in\cone(\mathcal{S})}}{\argmin}\!\,\|\theta-(\theta_{k}-\frac{1}{L}\nabla f(\theta_{k}))\|^{2}\!

3:

Variant 1:

\theta_{k+1}=\argmin_{\theta\in\cone(\mathcal{S})}f(\theta)

4:

Remove atoms with zero weights from
\mathcal{S}

At each iteration, Algorithm
13
maintains the set of active atoms
\mathcal{S}
(
i.e.
the atoms with non-zero weight forming the iterate) by adding
z_{k}
and removing atoms with zero weights after the update.
In Variant 0, the algorithm minimizes the quadratic upper bound
g_{\theta_{k}}(\theta)
on
f
at
\theta_{k}
imitating a gradient descent step with projection onto a “varying” target, i.e.,
\cone(\mathcal{S})
.
In Variant 1, the original objective
f
is minimized over
\cone(\mathcal{S})
at each iteration, which is in general more efficient than minimizing
f
over
\cone(\mathcal{A})
if
k
is small.

For
f(\theta)=\frac{1}{2}\|y-\theta\|^{2}
,
y\in\mathcal{H}
, Variant 1 recovers Algorithm 1 in
[
YWD15
]
and the OMP variant in
[
BEZ08
]
which both only apply to this specific objective.

22.1
Linear Rates

The linear rate case is interesting, as it turns out that the rate is surprisingly closer in spirit to the one of Frank-Wolfe
[
LJJ15
]
rather than the one of MP
[
Loc+17
]
. To illustrate these relations, we only discuss the non-affine invariant linear rate in this dissertaiton and refer to
[
Loc+17a
]
for the remaining affine invariant case.

We start by recalling some of the geometric complexity quantities that were introduced in the context of FW by
[
LJJ15
]

and are adapted here to the optimization problem we aim to solve (minimization over
\cone(\mathcal{A})
instead of
\conv(\mathcal{A})
).

Directional Width
[
LJJ15
]

The directional width of a set
\mathcal{A}
w.r.t. a direction
r\in\mathcal{H}
is defined as:

\displaystyle dirW(\mathcal{A},r):=\max_{s,v\in\mathcal{A}}\big\langle\tfrac{r}{\|r\|},s-v\big\rangle

(41)

Pyramidal Directional Width
[
LJJ15
]

The Pyramidal Directional Width of a set
\mathcal{A}
with respect to a direction
r
and a reference point
\theta\in\conv(\mathcal{A})
is defined as:

\displaystyle PdirW(\mathcal{A},r,\theta):=\min_{\mathcal{S}\in\mathcal{S}_{\theta}}dirW(\mathcal{S}\cup\{s(\mathcal{A},r)\},r),

(42)

where
\mathcal{S}_{\theta}:=\{\mathcal{S}\ |\ \mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{A}
and
\theta
is a proper convex combination of all the elements in
\mathcal{S}\}
and
s(\mathcal{A},r):=\max_{s\in\mathcal{A}}\langle\frac{r}{\|r\|},s\rangle
.

Inspired by the notion of pyramidal width in
[
LJJ15
]
, which is the minimal pyramidal directional width computed over the set of feasible directions, we now define the cone width of a set
\mathcal{A}
where only the generating faces (
\gfaces
) of
\cone(\mathcal{A})
(instead of the faces of
\conv(\mathcal{A})
) are considered. Before doing so we introduce the notions of
face
,
generating faces
(
\gfaces
), and
feasible direction
.

Face of a Convex Set
[
LJJ15
]

Let us consider a set
\mathcal{K}
with a
k-
dimensional affine hull along with a point
\theta\in\mathcal{K}
. Then,
\mathcal{K}
is a
k-
dimensional face of
\conv(\mathcal{A})
if
\mathcal{K}=\conv(\mathcal{A})\cap\{y\colon\langle r,y-\theta\rangle=0\}
for some normal vector
r
and
\conv(A)
is contained in the half-space determined by
r
, i.e.,
\langle r,y-\theta\rangle\leq 0
,
\forall\ y\in\conv(\mathcal{A})
.
Intuitively, given a set
\conv(\mathcal{A})
one can think of
\conv(\mathcal{A})
being a
\mathrm{dim}(\conv(\mathcal{A}))-
dimensional face of itself, an edge on the border of the set a
1
-dimensional face and a vertex a
0
-dimensional face.

Face of a Cone and
\gfaces

Similarly, a
k-
dimensional face of a cone is an open and unbounded set
\cone(\mathcal{A})\cap\{y\colon\langle r,y-\theta\rangle=0\}
for some normal vector
r
and
\cone(A)
is contained in the half space determined by
r
. We can define the generating faces of a cone as:

\displaystyle\gfaces(\cone(\mathcal{A}))\!:=\!\left\{\mathcal{B}\cap\conv(\mathcal{A})\colon\!\mathcal{B}\in\faces(\cone(\mathcal{A}))\right\}.

Note that
\gfaces(\cone(\mathcal{A}))\subset\faces(\conv(\mathcal{A}))
and
\conv(\mathcal{A})\in\gfaces(\cone(\mathcal{A}))
.
Furthermore, for each
\mathcal{K}\in\gfaces(\cone(\mathcal{A}))
,
\cone(\mathcal{K})
is a
k-
dimensional face of
\cone(\mathcal{A})
. An intuitive visualization of
\gfaces
is depicted in Figure
5
(right).

Figure 5
:
(left) Visualization of the atomic norm. It corresponds to the smallest constant
c
so that a point
x
belong to
c\cdot\conv(\mathcal{A})
. If the origin is not in the relative interior the norm is not well defined everywhere. (right) Visualization of the
\gfaces
of a
\cone
generated by a convex set.

We now adapt the notion of
feasible directions
from
[
LJJ15
]
to the cone setting. A direction
d
is feasible from
\theta\in\cone(\mathcal{A})
if it points inwards
\cone(\mathcal{A})
, i.e., if
\exists\varepsilon>0
s.t.
\theta+\varepsilon d\in\cone(\mathcal{A})
.
Since a face of the cone is itself a cone, if a direction is feasible from
\theta\in\cone(\mathcal{K})\setminus 0
, it is feasible from every positive rescaling of
\theta
. We therefore can consider only the feasible directions on the generating faces (which are closed and bounded sets). Finally, we define the cone width of
\mathcal{A}
.

Cone Width

\displaystyle\cw:=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{K}\in\gfaces(\cone(\mathcal{A}))\\
\theta\in\mathcal{K}\\
r\in\cone(\mathcal{K}-\theta)\setminus\{0\}\end{subarray}}PdirW(\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{A},r,\theta)

(43)

We are now ready to show the linear convergence of Algorithms
12
and
13
.

Theorem 23
.

Let
